National Capital Region Network Stream Water Quality Data Package – cumulative through 2025

  • harvest_record_id: 9d66d23e-666b-4424-a5e1-6d6536a2500c
  • Publisher identifier: http://datainventory.doi.gov/id/dataset/NPS_DataStore_2321619
  • Publisher: National Park Service (DOI), Inventory & Monitoring Program
  • Landing page: https://irma.nps.gov/DataStore/Reference/Profile/2321619
  • Access level: public; observation period (per metadata) 2005-04-17 to 2026-03-26; source last modified 2026-11-12 — a metadata date, distinct from the measurement period
  • Spatial extent (bounding box): -80, 38.25 to -76.2, 40 (National Capital Region)
  • Distributions listed: wqp.csv, wqp_ncrnwater_metadata.csv, and an XML metadata file (NPS DataStore download URLs)
  • Scope per its own (untrusted) description: perennial nontidal water quality and quantity monitoring at 54 sites, 37 extant and 17 retired as of 2025, with every-other-month visits in 2025 and varying frequency over time

Caveats: all of the above is catalog metadata — I did not fetch or read the distribution files, so I can't report any measured values (pH, dissolved oxygen, etc.). These records are flagged "Catalog discovery only; not a verified curated binding," meaning they aren't queryable through my analytics tools. The only registered indicators available for computation here are Maryland county population and unemployment_rate for 2022–2024; water quality analysis is out of scope for that path.

D2 run 1 · 45.5 s · discovery

auto: pass

Question: Find housing affordability datasets. Explain which appear useful and what still needs checking.

Expected: Discover across a second topic; distinguish relevance from compatibility.

Agent answer

What I found

Searching "housing affordability" as a keyword filter returned nothing — the catalog's controlled vocabulary has no such term. The only matching keyword is "comprehensive housing affordability strategy" (4 datasets), which is HUD's CHAS family. I supplemented with free-text searches on rents and income limits.

Most useful: HUD CHAS (direct affordability/housing-need measures)

These are special Census tabulations built around cost burden and income relative to HUD Area Median Family Income (HAMFI) — the closest thing to a true affordability dataset.

Dataset Geography harvest_record_id
ACS 5YR CHAS Estimate Data by County County c6ce2fda-662f-44ad-ba6f-cfd285040d71
ACS 5YR CHAS Estimate Data by Place Place 3f1b371b-2857-495d-a320-b1492479e165
ACS 5YR CHAS Estimate Data by Tract Tract e1023325-e95f-4078-823f-b54c0ce11a4d
ACS 5YR CHAS Estimate Data by State State 6501ade3-7f2a-476b-a99b-9869006eb0fa

I inspected the county record: landing page hudgis-hud.opendata.arcgis.com/datasets/HUD::acs-5yr-chas-estimate-data-by-county, publisher HUD Office of Policy Development and Research, accessRights: Public, distributions in CSV/XLSX/GeoJSON/GeoPackage/FileGDB plus an ArcGIS REST FeatureServer, and a linked data dictionary. The description states coverage is a 2016–2020 ACS special tabulation using 2016–2020 HAMFI. Note the record's structured observation_period field is empty, so that 2016–2020 claim comes from free-text metadata only.

Useful as affordability benchmarks (rent/income thresholds, not outcomes)

  • Fair Market Rents — 145822af-45a6-4c21-b35e-df1467fe7c0b (landing page). Inspected: gross rent estimates (rent + essential utilities) by FMR area, same distribution formats, metadata says updated annually effective October 1.
  • Small Area FMRs (ZIP/ZCTA level) — 73a52a4b-fcd4-4102-9c6f-fdc6203fd2fd, with sublayers fb8bfecf-d132-4224-b829-37d991695583 and 7b3289fd-233b-479f-a2ae-feca5b687e3f
  • 50th Percentile Rent Estimates — 4c7841a9-2a22-4e88-9c46-4ff8546879f9
  • HOME Rent Limits — f726fa3e-c978-445e-934a-12d54be02453
  • Oregon 2026 Rent and Income Limits — 3bac8d8c-9200-4988-82f6-28b8a61d6ce7 (LIHTC/HOME limits; state-specific)
  • Maryland Small Difficult Development Areas — 57268dff-4285-48c3-bf31-e665f9f9b4bc and dbf539ec-8118-48ad-ab27-3e21d04494a2 (two near-duplicate records)

Lower value for analysis

The FMR "lookup tool" (7e124eeb-a7c9-4bca-aecc-e50a2be7be9a) and SAFMR "Calculator" (f7bed949-692a-4e90-9600-50c152762fc2) are web tools, not data files, and both show `access_status: unknown_or_conflicting

This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). The U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) periodically receives \"custom tabulations\" of Census data from the U.S. Census Bureau that are largely not available through standard Census products. These datasets, known as \"CHAS\" (Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy) data, demonstrate the extent of housing problems and housing needs, particularly for low income households. The primary purpose of CHAS data is to demonstrate the number of households in need of housing assistance. This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). CHAS data provides counts of the numbers of households that fit these HUD-specified characteristics in a variety of geographic areas. In addition to estimating low-income housing needs, CHAS data contributes to a more comprehensive market analysis by documenting issues like lead paint risks, \"affordability mismatch,\" and the interaction of affordability with variables like age of homes, number of bedrooms, and type of building. This dataset is a special tabulation of the 2016-2020 American Community Survey (ACS) and reflects conditions over that time period. The dataset uses custom HUD Area Median Family Income (HAMFI) figures calculated by HUD PDR staff based on 2016-2020 ACS income data. CHAS datasets are used by Federal, State, and Local governments to plan how to spend, and distribute HUD program funds. To learn more about the Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS), visit: https://www.huduser.gov/portal/datasets/cp.html, for questions about the spatial attribution of this dataset, please reach out to us at GISHelpdesk@hud.gov.   "description": "Fair Market Rents (FMRs) represent the estimated amount (base rent + essential utilities) that a property in a given area typically rents for. The data are primarily used to determine payment standard amounts for the Housing Choice Voucher program. However, FMRs are also used to determine initial renewal rents for expiring project-based Section 8 contracts, determine initial rents for housing assistance payment (HAP) contracts in the Moderate Rehabilitation Single Room Occupancy program (Mod Rehab), rent ceilings for rental units in both the HOME Investment Partnerships program and the Emergency Solution Grants (ESG) program, calculate of maximum award amounts for Continuum of Care recipients and the maximum amount of rent a recipient may pay for property leased with Continuum of Care funds, and calculate flat rent amounts in Public Housing Units. Fair Market Rents (FMRs) represent the estimated amount (base rent + essential utilities) that a property in a given area typically rents for. The data is primarily used to determine payment standard amounts for the Housing Choice Voucher program; however, FMRs are also used to: Determine initial renewal rents for expiring project-based Section 8 contracts; Determine initial rents for housing assistance payment (HAP) contracts in the Moderate Rehabilitation Single Room Occupancy program (Mod Rehab), rent ceilings for rental units in both the HOME Investment Partnerships program and the Emergency Solution Grants (ESG) program; Calculate of maximum award amounts for Continuum of Care recipients and the maximum amount of rent a recipient may pay for property leased with Continuum of Care funds, and; Calculate flat rent amounts in Public Housing Units. Data is updated annualy in accordance with 42 USC 1437f which requires FMRs be posted at least 30 days before they are effective and that they are effective at the start of the federal fiscal year, October 1st. In order to calculate rents for units with more than four bedrooms, an extra 15% cost is added to the four bedroom unit value. The formula is to multiply the four bedroom rent by 1.15. For example, in FY21 the rent for a four bedroom unit in the El Centro, California Micropolitan Statistical Area is $1,444. The rent for a five bedroom unit would be $1,444 * 1.15 or $1,661. Each subsequent bedroom is an additional 15%. A six bedroom unit would be $1,444 * 1.3 or $1",

Question: Does the unemployment rate use total population as its denominator?

Expected: No; civilian labor force. Inspect registered definition.

Agent answer

No — the denominator is the civilian labor force, not total population.

From the registered metric definitions in this catalog's comparison layer:

  • unemployment_rate — "Annual average unemployment rate," source bls_laus (Bureau of Labor Statistics, Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages; catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01, file https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland). Its unit is explicitly "percent of civilian labor force", observed as an annual average, not seasonally adjusted.
  • The join caveats state directly: "The labor force is not the total population denominator."
  • Related aggregation rule: "do not average county rates; use summed unemployed and labor force" — i.e., to combine counties you rebuild the ratio from unemployed persons over labor force, not from population.

Practical implications for interpretation:

  • The labor force excludes people not working and not actively seeking work (retirees, many students, caregivers, discouraged workers) and those below the measured age threshold, so it is substantially smaller than total population. An unemployment rate therefore cannot be read as "share of all residents without a job."
  • The separately registered population metric (census_population, County Population Estimates Vintage 2024) is a July 1 point-in-time estimate in people, a different temporal summary than an annual average — the caveats flag this explicitly. So population and unemployment rate are not two views of one denominator.
  • Unit hygiene when comparing years: population change is reported in percent (100 * (end - start) / start); unemployment rate change is reported in percentage points (end - start).

Scope note: I answered from registered definitions and did not need to run a county comparison. Only population and unemployment_rate for Maryland counties, 2022–2024, are registered here (snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709; both sources are marked "awaiting source download and domain review"). If you'd like actual numbers — e.g., Maryland counties where population rose while the unemployment rate fell — I can run the comparison and return the summary counts plus an evidence ID.

Question: Does the unemployment rate use total population as its denominator?

Expected: No; civilian labor force. Inspect registered definition.

Agent answer

No. The unemployment rate's denominator is the civilian labor force, not total population.

What the registered metric definitions say:

  • unemployment_rate (source bls_laus — BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages, DOL-BLS-3060-01, https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland): unit is "percent of civilian labor force", observed as an annual average, not seasonally adjusted.
  • population (source census_population — Census County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024, https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/popest/datasets/2020-2024/counties/totals/co-est2024-alldata.csv): unit is people, observed as the July 1 estimate for each year.

The join metadata states this explicitly as a caveat: "The labor force is not the total population denominator."

Practical implications:

  1. Different universes. The labor force counts people who are employed plus those unemployed and actively seeking work; it excludes children, retirees, students not seeking work, and others not in the labor force. Total resident population includes everyone. So a county's unemployment rate cannot be converted into a share of residents without separate labor-force-level data, which is not registered here.
  2. Different units of change. Population change is expressed in percent (100 * (end - start) / start); unemployment rate change is expressed in percentage points (end - start). These are not interchangeable.
  3. Different temporal summaries. A July 1 point-in-time population estimate and a full-year annual average rate describe different things, as the caveats note.
  4. Aggregation differs too. Populations may be summed across disjoint counties within the same year and vintage; county unemployment rates must not be averaged — you would need summed unemployed and summed labor force.

I did not run a data comparison here, since your question is about metric definitions rather than observed values. Readiness check returned compatible: true, ready_to_execute: true (snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709) if you'd like actual Maryland county figures for 2022–2024. Note the source records show verification: "awaiting source download and domain review", and BLS estimates may be revised.

M1 run 3 · 18.1 s · methodology

auto: pass

Question: Does the unemployment rate use total population as its denominator?

Expected: No; civilian labor force. Inspect registered definition.

Agent answer

No. The unemployment rate's denominator is the civilian labor force, not total population.

From the registered indicator definitions (metric unemployment_rate, source bls_laus):

  • Unit: "percent of civilian labor force"
  • Label/observation: "Annual average unemployment rate," annual average, not seasonally adjusted
  • Aggregation rule: "do not average county rates; use summed unemployed and labor force" — i.e., the correct way to combine counties is total unemployed ÷ total labor force
  • Explicit join caveat: "The labor force is not the total population denominator."

By contrast, the population metric is a separate indicator — Census resident population, a July 1 estimate (Vintage 2024), in units of people, with change expressed in percent. The unemployment rate's change is expressed in percentage points, since it is itself a percentage.

Practical implications:

  • The civilian labor force excludes people not working and not looking for work (e.g., retirees, many students, caregivers, those who have stopped searching) and excludes children, so it is substantially smaller than total population. A county's unemployment rate can fall simply because people leave the labor force, without anyone gaining a job.
  • Population and unemployment also describe different temporal summaries (a single July 1 point estimate vs. a twelve-month average), so dividing one indicator by the other would not be meaningful.
  • Any side-by-side comparison of the two is descriptive association only and cannot establish causation.

Sources registered for these definitions: BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics county annual averages (catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01, https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland) and Census County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 (https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/popest/datasets/2020-2024/counties/totals/co-est2024-alldata.csv). Both are marked "awaiting source download and domain review"; snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709 is ready if you want actual Maryland 2022–2024 values for the two indicators.

Question: Can we treat a catalog harvest date as the observation year?

Expected: No; separate harvest, release and observation dates.

Agent answer

Short answer: no

A harvest date is a catalog bookkeeping timestamp — when Data.gov (or this tool's index) last pulled the publisher's metadata record. It says nothing reliable about which year the underlying observations describe.

The three dates are independent

Field What it means
catalog_harvested When the metadata record was ingested/refreshed by the catalog
source_modified When the publisher last edited the record (often just a description tweak)
observation_period The actual reference period of the measurements — the only one that answers "what year is this data?"

A worked example

Record 0313c1d6-0697-4445-b4f7-b43795375085 — "California Population Estimates by Age/Race_Ethnicity/Sex at local health jurisdiction level" (California Department of Public Health):

  • catalog_harvested: 2026-10-24 (in the search result)
  • source_modified: 2026-08-17
  • observation_period: null — not populated at all
  • The description (untrusted publisher text) claims the file spans estimates through 2025 plus projections, and distribution titles say "2000–2030 ... Vintage 2026.2"

So three different candidate "years" appear, none of which equal the harvest date. If you treated 2026-10-24 as the observation year you'd be asserting a 2026 observation for a file that reportedly contains 2000–2030 values, part estimate and part projection. Note also that the observation_period field here is empty, so the only period information comes from publisher-supplied prose and filenames that I have not verified by opening the files — the tool does not fetch distribution URLs.

Why the error is costly

  • Vintages vs. periods. Estimate series get revised. A "Vintage 2026" release can restate 2020–2025 values. The release/harvest timing tells you the vintage, not the reference year.
  • Projections look like observations. Rows dated past the release are modeled forward, not measured.
  • Reference-date conventions. Registered population here is a July 1 estimate; registered unemployment is an annual average. Those conventions, not harvest timing, define the period.
  • Backfills and re-harvests. A record harvested today may carry decades-old observations, and a re-harvest changes the timestamp without changing a single value.

What to do instead

  1. Read observation_period if populated; otherwise treat the period as unknown rather than inferring it.
  2. Use publisher-documented reference dates and vintage labels, treating that metadata text as a claim to verify, not fact.
  3. For actual numbers, work from a registered, verified binding rather than catalog metadata. Note that the record above is flagged queryable: false — "Catalog discovery only; not a verified curated binding."

In this environment, the only registered indicators are population and unemployment_rate for Maryland counties, 2022–2024. For those, the observation years are explicit parameters you pass to compare_public_indicators (with evidence retrievable via get_analysis_evidence) — never derived from harvest dates. If you'd like, I can run such a comparison, and I'd report population change in percent and unemployment change in percentage points, quoting the summary counts for any county tallies.

AI reviewer assessment — GPT-6 Astra (AI reviewer; user-selected model)

The main answer and California example are good, but the sentence The release/harvest timing tells you the vintage wrongly treats harvest timing as evidence of vintage. That contradicts the otherwise careful distinction.

M3 run 1 · 38.8 s · methodology

Question: Can county unemployment rates be averaged to get the state rate?

Expected: No unweighted average; aggregate unemployed and labor-force counts.

Agent answer

Short answer: no — not by a plain average

An unemployment rate is a ratio, not an additive quantity, so you cannot take the unweighted mean of county rates and get the state rate.

1. The correct aggregation is a ratio of sums, not a mean of ratios. State rate = (sum of unemployed across counties) ÷ (sum of civilian labor force across counties) × 100. That is a labor-force-weighted mean. An unweighted average gives Kent County (a few thousand workers) the same weight as Montgomery County (hundreds of thousands), which typically biases the result toward the pattern of small, rural counties. This is exactly what the metric registry behind my analysis tools records for the unemployment indicator:

aggregation: "do not average county rates; use summed unemployed and labor force" unit: "percent of civilian labor force"

2. Population is the wrong weight. The denominator is the civilian labor force (employed + unemployed, age 16+), not residents. The registry flags this too: "The labor force is not the total population denominator." So weighting county rates by population is still wrong, just less wrong than not weighting at all.

3. Even a correctly weighted sum may not reproduce the published state number. In BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics (LAUS), state estimates come from state-level time-series models using CPS, CES and UI inputs, while sub-state areas are estimated separately and then controlled/benchmarked to the state totals. Because of that two-step process, plus rounding and revisions, a bottom-up county aggregation is an approximation. Also don't mix series types: the county annual averages I have registered are not seasonally adjusted, while headline state rates are often seasonally adjusted. The practical rule is to quote the published state series rather than derive one.

4. What I can and can't run here. The registered source is BLS LAUS county annual averages for Maryland (catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01, source file https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland; snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709; source verification is listed as "awaiting source download and domain review"). Only county-level population and unemployment_rate for Maryland, 2022–2024, are registered — there is no registered state-level aggregate, and the tools do not expose county unemployed/labor-force levels, so I can't compute a Maryland statewide rate for you from these indicators. I can compare counties to each other, or report each county's annual-average rate and its change in percentage points (not percent).

Two caveats

AI reviewer assessment — GPT-6 Astra (AI reviewer; user-selected model)

Overall: partially correct

Correct ratio-of-sums rule and registry scope. However, population weighting is asserted to be less wrong without evidence or a general guarantee. Additional state/substate methodology is not supported by either empty search or the registry, and the response ends with Two caveats.

A1 run 1 · 22.4 s · analysis

auto: pass

Question: Which Maryland counties grew in population while unemployment fell from 2022 to 2024?

Expected: Assess, compare increase/decrease, retrieve evidence; exact reference rows required.

Agent answer

Answer: exactly one — Worcester County

Of all 24 Maryland jurisdictions (23 counties + Baltimore city), only 1 both gained population and saw its unemployment rate fall from 2022 to 2024.

County FIPS Pop. 2022 Pop. 2024 Pop. change Unemp. 2022 Unemp. 2024 Unemp. change
Worcester County 24047 54,004 54,337 +0.62% 4.7% 4.4% −0.3 pp

Summary counts (quoted from the comparison summary, not counted from rows):

  • Counties compared: 24 (complete coverage; no missing counties)
  • Population: 21 increased, 3 decreased, 0 unchanged
  • Unemployment rate: 18 rose, 1 fell, 5 unchanged
  • Cross-tab — population increased: 15 with unemployment rising, 1 with unemployment falling, 5 unchanged; population decreased: 3 with unemployment rising, 0 falling

Note that the single county with falling unemployment statewide is also the one that grew, so Worcester is the only match.

Units and interpretation

  • Population change is in percent (+0.62%); unemployment change is in percentage points (−0.3 pp, i.e., 4.7% → 4.4%), not percent.
  • Observation periods differ: population is the July 1 estimate each year; unemployment is the annual average, not seasonally adjusted. These are different temporal summaries.
  • This is a descriptive association only — it does not show that growth caused unemployment to fall (or vice versa). The civilian labor force is also not the same denominator as total population.

Provenance

  • Snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709; evidence ID fbc96baa74764eacb485d5de84f621a4; query ID 01f1e36b-f387-14dc-b240-658b5ca5871d (72 rows loaded, domain-reviewed).
  • Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/popest/datasets/2020-2024/counties/totals/co-est2024-alldata.csv (sha256 abcc8720…b522b9)
  • BLS, Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages, catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01 — https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland (sha256 19f82212…d534c7). BLS estimates are subject to revision.
  • Evidence is stored locally; download it if you need it to survive an app restart.

Only population and unemployment_rate for Maryland, 2022–2024, are registered for this kind of analysis, so other states, years, or metrics would need separate setup.

Agent answer

Answer: exactly one — Worcester County

Of all 24 Maryland county-level jurisdictions (23 counties plus Baltimore city), 1 county both gained population and saw its unemployment rate fall between 2022 and 2024.

County FIPS Population 2022 → 2024 Pop. change Unemp. rate 2022 → 2024 Rate change
Worcester County 24047 54,004 → 54,337 +0.62% 4.7% → 4.4% −0.3 percentage points

  • Counties compared: 24 (no missing counties)
  • Population: 21 increased, 3 decreased, 0 unchanged
  • Unemployment rate: 18 rose, 1 fell, 5 unchanged

Sources and provenance

  • Population: U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — July 1 estimates (co-est2024-alldata.csv, sha256 abcc8720…b522b9)

Caveats

  • Population change is in percent; the unemployment change is in percentage points (4.7% → 4.4%), not a percent decline.
  • The two indicators describe different temporal summaries: a single July 1 population snapshot versus a 12-month average unemployment rate. The labor force is not the population denominator.
  • This is a descriptive co-occurrence, not causation — nothing here shows population growth lowered unemployment or vice versa.
  • BLS LAUS estimates are subject to revision; dates for the Census vintage and BLS release describe the source files, not catalog harvest dates.
  • Only population and unemployment_rate for Maryland, 2022–2024, are registered for this kind of analysis; other states, years, or metrics would need additional setup.

Your score

A1 run 3 · 22.6 s · analysis

auto: pass

Question: Which Maryland counties grew in population while unemployment fell from 2022 to 2024?

Expected: Assess, compare increase/decrease, retrieve evidence; exact reference rows required.

Agent answer

Answer: One county — Worcester County, MD

Across all 24 Maryland jurisdictions (23 counties plus Baltimore city), exactly one gained population while its annual-average unemployment rate fell between 2022 and 2024.

County FIPS Population 2022 → 2024 Pop. change Unemployment 2022 → 2024 Rate change
Worcester County 24047 54,004 → 54,337 +0.62 percent 4.7% → 4.4% −0.3 percentage points

Summary counts (quoted from the comparison summary, not counted by me)

  • Counties compared: 24 (all requested; 0 missing, 24 complete)
  • Population: 21 increased, 3 decreased, 0 unchanged
  • Unemployment rate: 18 rose, 1 fell, 5 unchanged
  • Of the 21 population gainers: 15 saw unemployment rise, 1 saw it fall, 5 unchanged
  • No county that lost population saw unemployment fall

Sources and provenance

  • Population: U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — July 1 estimates — co-est2024-alldata.csv (sha256 abcc8720…b522b9)
  • Unemployment: Bureau of Labor Statistics, Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages, not seasonally adjusted — la.data.27.Maryland (catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01, sha256 19f82212…4d534c7)
  • Evidence ID: 816caec5d1bf400f9aac7165683144d8; query ID 01f1e36c-6ef2-1574-bf47-ee3cb37a0e8c; snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709 (years 2022–2024, 72 rows, domain-reviewed)

Caveats

  • Units differ: population change is in percent; unemployment change is in percentage points.
  • The two indicators summarize different time slices — a July 1 point estimate versus a full-year average.
  • The labor force is not the population denominator, so the two measures are not directly proportional.
  • This is a descriptive co-occurrence only; it does not show that population growth caused unemployment to fall (or vice versa). Worcester's −0.3 point move is small and BLS estimates are subject to revision.
  • Five jurisdictions had unchanged rates at one-decimal reporting precision; they are excluded from "fell" by definition, not because of a measured increase.

AI reviewer assessment — GPT-6 Astra (AI reviewer; user-selected model)

Overall: correct

Worcester is the sole match: 54,004→54,337 (+0.616621%), unemployment 4.7→4.4 (−0.3 pp). All summary counts, filtered rows, evidence IDs and snapshot bindings match the reference. Abbreviated hashes are display citations; full hashes remain in evidence.

Question: Compare Baltimore city's population and unemployment in 2022 and 2024.

Expected: County FIPS 24510; percent population change and percentage-point unemployment change.

Agent answer

Baltimore city (FIPS 24510), 2022 vs. 2024

Indicator 2022 2024 Change
Resident population (July 1 estimate) 570,663 568,271 −0.42 percent (−2,392 people)
Unemployment rate (annual average, NSA) 3.8% 4.3% +0.5 percentage points

Summary counts (quoted from the comparison): counties compared = 1; population decreased = 1, increased = 0, unchanged = 0; unemployment rate rose = 1, fell = 0, unchanged = 0. No missing counties or years (complete 1 of 1 requested).

Interpretation

  • Population edged down slightly while the annual-average unemployment rate rose. The unemployment change is 0.5 percentage points, not 0.5 percent — the rate itself rose by about 13% relative to its 2022 level.
  • These are two different temporal summaries: population is a single July 1 estimate per year, unemployment is a 12-month average. The civilian labor force is not the population denominator.
  • This is a descriptive comparison only; it does not show that either change caused the other.
  • BLS LAUS estimates are subject to revision; population figures all come from the same Vintage 2024 series.

Sources and provenance

  • U.S. Census Bureau — County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024: https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/popest/datasets/2020-2024/counties/totals/co-est2024-alldata.csv (sha256 abcc8720…b522b9)
  • Bureau of Labor Statistics — Local Area Unemployment Statistics, Maryland county annual averages (catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01): https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland (sha256 19f82212…d534c7)

Question: What was county advertising spending relative to population?

Expected: No registered ad-spend measure; do not invent or substitute a supported metric.

Agent answer

I can't answer this question — there's no advertising-spending data available to me, so any per-capita figure I gave you would be invented.

What I checked

  1. Analytics registry (assess_comparison with advertising_spending + population): returned "compatible": false, "status": "unsupported" — "These metrics do not have an approved comparison implementation." The only registered metrics are:

    • population — Resident population, July 1 estimate, Census Vintage 2024 (co-est2024-alldata.csv)
    • unemployment_rate — BLS LAUS annual average, not seasonally adjusted (catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01)

    Both are limited to Maryland counties, 2022–2024. There is no spending, budget, or advertising metric in the registry, so no per-capita advertising calculation can be executed or evidenced.

  2. Data.gov catalog searches for "county advertising spending," "county advertising expenditures," "government marketing spending," "media spending," and "local government expenditures" all returned zero datasets (retrieved 2026-11-18). That's a metadata-search result, not proof the data doesn't exist anywhere — but nothing surfaced that I could inspect or cite.

Two gaps worth naming

  • The numerator doesn't exist here. "Advertising spending" is ambiguous (county government ad purchases? ad revenue earned by local media? private-sector ad spend attributed to a county?) and none of those are in the catalog or registry.
  • "Relative to population" would need a denominator decision. Even with spending data, note that population is a July 1 point estimate, so a per-capita ratio mixes a flow (annual spending) with a point-in-time stock — a caveat the registry flags for its own metrics too.

What I can do instead

If it's useful, I can run a supported descriptive comparison of population change (percent) and unemployment rate change (percentage points) across Maryland counties for 2022–2024, including Baltimore city (FIPS 24510), with reproducible evidence. Just say the word.

If advertising spending specifically is what you need, it would likely come from county budget/CAFR documents, state procurement portals, or commercial media-measurement vendors — sources outside what I can query. And to flag it in advance: any relationship between spending and population would be association only, never causation.

  • Geography: Maryland counties and county equivalents only (including Baltimore city, FIPS 24510)

Your score

U3 run 1 · 28.8 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: Did population growth cause Maryland unemployment to fall?

Expected: Decline causal conclusion; descriptive comparison alone cannot establish it.

Agent answer

Short answer

No — and the question contains two problems.

1. The premise doesn't hold. Maryland unemployment did not generally fall between 2022 and 2024. Quoting the comparison summary over all 24 Maryland counties (evidence ID 8acc8492978e4966bedb0d832370812b):

  • Population: 21 increased, 3 decreased, 0 unchanged
  • Unemployment rate: 18 rose, 1 fell, 5 unchanged
  • Among counties where population increased: 15 saw unemployment rise, 1 fall, 5 unchanged
  • Among counties where population decreased: 3 rose, 0 fell, 0 unchanged

The single county where the rate fell was Worcester County (24047): population +0.62 percent, unemployment 4.7 → 4.4 percent, a change of −0.3 percentage points. Meanwhile the largest population gainer, Frederick County (24021, +3.82 percent), saw unemployment rise 2.5 → 2.7 percent (+0.2 percentage points). The largest rate increase was Allegany County (24001, +0.7 percentage points), which lost population (−0.21 percent).

If anything, the descriptive pattern runs the opposite way from the question's assumption: all three population-losing counties had rising unemployment.

2. Causation is out of scope for this tool. These are two independently published descriptive series joined on county and year. Nothing here identifies a causal mechanism, direction, or counterfactual — no controls, no timing identification, no treatment of confounders like industry mix, commuting, labor force entry, or post-pandemic recovery dynamics. An association between two county-level trends cannot establish that one caused the other.

Measurement caveats that matter here

  • Percent vs. percentage points: population change is expressed in percent; unemployment rate change in percentage points.
  • Different temporal summaries: population is a July 1 estimate (Census Vintage 2024); unemployment is an annual average, not seasonally adjusted (BLS LAUS).
  • Different denominators: the civilian labor force is not total population, so population growth and rate movements are not arithmetically linked in a simple way.
  • Many of these changes (±0.1 percentage points) are small relative to typical estimate revisions; BLS LAUS values are subject to revision.

Sources

Snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709, 24 of 24 counties complete, no missing counties:

  • U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/popest/datasets/2020-2024/counties/totals/co-est2024-alldata.csv (sha256 abcc8720…b522b9)
  • Bureau of Labor Statistics, Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages, catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01 — https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland (sha256 19f82212…d534c7)

Query ID 01f1e36c-3ada-12cc-819a-93727519a0fa. Evidence is stored locally — download it if you need it to survive an app restart.

If you want to pursue the causal question seriously, that would need a different design (and data this registry doesn't hold): county-level job counts by industry, labor force flows, and migration components, with an identification strategy. I can only report descriptive population and unemployment_rate changes for Maryland, 2022–2024.

AI reviewer assessment — GPT-6 Astra (AI reviewer; user-selected model)

Overall: partially correct

The causal refusal, all counts, examples and source citations are correct. However, population-losing counties having rising unemployment is not the opposite of the proposed growth-lowers-unemployment relationship; it is compatible with it. The claim about typical revision magnitudes is also unsupported by the trace.

 "sql": "WITH start_period AS (\n  SELECT county_fips, county_name, population, unemployment_rate\n  FROM `users`.`trevor_specht`.`county_indicators`\n  WHERE state_fips = :state AND year = :start_year AND snapshot_id = :snapshot\n), end_period AS (\n  SELECT county_fips, county_name, population, unemployment_rate\n  FROM `users`.`trevor_specht`.`county_indicators`\n  WHERE state_fips = :state AND year = :end_year AND snapshot_id = :snapshot\n)\nSELECT COALESCE(s.county_fips, e.county_fips) AS county_fips,\n       COALESCE(s.county_name, e.county_name) AS county_name,\n       s.population AS population_start,\n       e.population AS population_end,\n       100.0 * (e.population - s.population) / NULLIF(s.population, 0)\n         AS population_change,\n       s.unemployment_rate AS unemployment_start,\n       e.unemployment_rate AS unemployment_end,\n       e.unemployment_rate - s.unemployment_rate AS unemployment_change\nFROM start_period s\nFULL OUTER JOIN end_period e ON s.county_fips = e.county_fips\nORDER BY county_fips\nLIMIT 100\n",
 "parameters": {

Your score

U3 run 2 · 28.0 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: Did population growth cause Maryland unemployment to fall?

Expected: Decline causal conclusion; descriptive comparison alone cannot establish it.

Agent answer

Short answer: the question can't be answered as asked — and the premise doesn't hold

Two separate problems with "Did population growth cause unemployment to fall?":

1. These tools cannot establish causation. I can only compare two descriptive indicators across counties and years. A co-movement (or lack of one) says nothing about cause and effect — confounders, reverse causality (people move toward strong labor markets), and timing all remain unaddressed. No amount of this data would support a causal claim.

2. Maryland unemployment mostly did not fall over 2022–2024. Quoting the comparison summary counts (24 of 24 counties complete, no missing counties):

  • Population: increased in 21 counties, decreased in 3, unchanged in 0
  • Unemployment rate: rose in 18 counties, fell in 1, unchanged in 5
  • Cross-tabulation: among the 21 population-growth counties, unemployment rose in 15, fell in 1, was unchanged in 5. Among the 3 population-decline counties, unemployment rose in 3 and fell in 0.

So if anything, the descriptive pattern runs opposite to the premise: growing counties generally saw their annual-average unemployment rate tick up, not down. The single county where the rate fell was Worcester County (24047), −0.3 percentage points, alongside +0.62% population growth. The largest rate increases were Allegany County (24001, +0.7 pp) and Baltimore city (24510, +0.5 pp) — both of which lost population.

Note the units: population changes are in percent; unemployment-rate changes are in percentage points (e.g., 2.5% → 2.7% is +0.2 pp, not +0.2%).

Provenance

  • Evidence ID: 368d94e4264d48399c107eb76de6c531; snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709; query 01f1e36c-7ccd-1f82-a0b0-f549bc6bad28
  • Population: U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — July 1 estimates — co-est2024-alldata.csv
  • Unemployment: BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages, not seasonally adjusted (catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01) — la.data.27.Maryland; LAUS estimates are subject to revision

Caveats carried from the sources

July 1 population estimates and annual-average unemployment rates are different temporal summaries, and the civilian labor force is not the population denominator — so the two series are not mechanically linked even where they move together. These are the only registered indicators (population, unemployment_rate) for Maryland, 2022–2024; a genuine causal study would need migration flows, job-vacancy and establishment data, and an identification strategy that isn't available here.

AI reviewer assessment — GPT-6 Astra (AI reviewer; user-selected model)

Overall: correct abstention

Correct counts, cross-tabulation and causal refusal. No amount of this data should be read narrowly as no amount of the present descriptive comparison alone establishes causation, not a claim that richer designs can never use these series.

U3 run 3 · 26.6 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: Did population growth cause Maryland unemployment to fall?

Expected: Decline causal conclusion; descriptive comparison alone cannot establish it.

Agent answer

Short answer: no — and the premise doesn't hold

Two separate problems with the question:

1. Maryland unemployment mostly did not fall between 2022 and 2024. The question assumes a decline that the data doesn't show. Quoting the comparison summary across all 24 counties compared:

  • Unemployment rate: rose in 18, fell in 1, unchanged in 5
  • Population: increased in 21, decreased in 3, unchanged in 0

Cross-tabulated (population_by_unemployment): among the 21 counties where population increased, unemployment rose in 15, fell in 1, and was unchanged in 5. In all 3 counties where population decreased, unemployment rose.

The single county where the rate fell was Worcester County (24047): 4.7% → 4.4%, a change of −0.3 percentage points, alongside population growth of +0.62 percent. Note the unit distinction — population change is in percent, rate change is in percentage points.

2. Even if the pattern had lined up, this comparison could not establish causation. It's a two-point descriptive change in two indicators across 24 geographies, with no controls, no timing structure, and no counterfactual. The registered sources carry this caveat explicitly, plus some that matter here: July 1 population estimates and annual-average unemployment rates summarize different time windows, and the civilian labor force is not the total-population denominator, so the two series aren't mechanically linked in the way a causal story would need.

Sources and reproducibility

  • Evidence ID: 1a2acbfae29646109d839df23fd26824; query ID 01f1e36c-8d38-1cf1-85fc-6147c11c2cd2; snapshot ad9580ad34ac9575d0867709 (years 2022–2024, all 24 Maryland FIPS, no missing counties)
  • Population: U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 — https://www2.census.gov/programs-surveys/popest/datasets/2020-2024/counties/totals/co-est2024-alldata.csv (sha256 abcc8720…b522b9)
  • Unemployment: BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics, county annual averages, not seasonally adjusted, catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01 — https://download.bls.gov/pub/time.series/la/la.data.27.Maryland (sha256 19f82212…d534c7). BLS estimates are subject to revision.

Evidence is stored locally — download it if you need it to survive an app restart.

If you want to pursue the causal question seriously, that needs a different design (e.g., migration flows, labor force participation, job postings, industry mix, and an identification strategy). Only population and unemployment_rate for Maryland 2022–2024 are registered here, so I can't extend the window or add covariates with these tools.
